That reading fits the sūrah, because these are men with working ears and working tongues. They heard the Qur'an recited in the mosque and they talked about it in the market. What they refused was the hearing that leads to acceptance and the speech that carries the truth onward.

Al-Alūsī and the author of al-Baḥr al-Muḥīṭ pause over the words themselves, because the sūrah's Arabic is being read aloud every day and the sound matters. Deafness in the language is a blockage in the ear, a blockage that keeps sound from arriving, and the root carries the sense of something sealed shut — a sealed water bottle is described with the same word. Dumbness is a damage in the tongue that stops speech. The plural form used here is the heavy plural, the one Arabic reserves for a quality that has taken a person over, so the description is not of a moment of silence but of a settled state.

Al-Bayḍawī adds a note on how to take the whole sentence. The words are applied to these men by way of comparison rather than transfer, because they blocked their ears from listening to the truth, refused to let their tongues speak it and would not use their eyes on the signs in front of them. A person who disables all three instruments should not be surprised when he is described by their failure. Qushayrī puts it in the language of the inner life: deaf to the call of the Real with the ears of their hearts, dumb in the conversation with Him that belongs to the deepest self, blind to the signs of His ordering of events.

Think of a man who is handed earplugs, a blindfold and a pad of tape at the door of a briefing that decides his future, and who puts them on himself, one by one, while the room waits. Nobody in that room can help him afterwards, and the verse is written in the language of his own choice.

**WHY THEY WILL NOT TURN BACK**

and adds the third along with the verdict: ***“and blind, so they will never return ˹to the Right Path˺”***. The third faculty closes the set. Al-Jalālayn says they are blind to the path of guidance, so they cannot see it, and the sentence that follows explains the consequence: they will not come back from their error. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the last words the same way: they do not return from their disbelief and their straying. tafsir_initial notes that the commentators name the realities these men have lost — the good, the truth and guidance (al-Ṭabarī) — and that the return spoken of here is the return to the guidance the sūrah mentioned a few verses earlier.

The Qur'an returns to this description in many places, and tafsir_initial gathers a few of them: (2:171 — **“the example of the disbelievers is like a shepherd who calls out to ˹sheep˺ that hear nothing but a shout and a cry”**), (17:97 — **“We will gather them on the Day of Judgment prone, blind, dumb, and deaf”**), and the verses that speak of hearts that do not understand ((6:25; 6:39; 10:42-43)). The same three failures appear at the resurrection, which is the sūrah's way of saying that a person's inner condition in this life is the shape he will be raised in.

Kashānī connects the blindness to a verse that describes a wall rather than an illness: (36:9 — **“We have put a barrier before them and a barrier behind them, and have covered them up so they cannot see”**). The image of a man shut inside a corridor explains why the verse ends where it does. This is not a man who cannot find the road. He is standing on it with his back turned.

The two verses of comparison also end here, and the reader should notice the sequence they complete. The first picture left the men unable to see. This verse says they are deaf, dumb and blind, and that they will not return. The next picture begins with rain, thunder and lightning, and shows men who can still hear enough to be frightened. The Book has moved from a man in total darkness to a man who is still being shaken.

---
## Verse 2:19

> Or ˹those caught in˺ a rainstorm from the sky with darkness, thunder, and lightning. They press their fingers into their ears at the sound of every thunder-clap for fear of death. And Allah encompasses the disbelievers ˹by His might˺

**THE STORM THEY WERE CAUGHT IN**

The second picture arrives with a storm in it: ***“Or ˹those caught in˺ a rainstorm from the sky with darkness, thunder”*** Maʿārif al-Qurʾān explains the structure of the passage. The sūrah has given two comparisons for the hypocrites, and the word or here opens the second one. The first compared them to a man who lit a fire and lost the light. The second compares them to travellers caught in a storm. The change of picture matches a change of people. The fire describes the man whose rejection has gone deep and who only carries the name of a believer for what it brings him. The storm describes the man who sees the truth, wants it at some moments and cannot bring himself to pay its price, so he moves a step forward and stops again.

Al-Mukhtaṣar gives the reading of the details that most of the commentators share. The rain from the sky stands for the Qur'an coming down. The thunder stands for its warnings. The lightning stands for the flashes of truth that appear to them from time to time. Ibn ʿAbbās works the same three items through the verse and adds the texture: what came down from God contains darkness in the reports of trials and turmoil, thunder in the rebukes that put fear into a listener, and lightning in the explanations, the instruction and the promises. A single Book carries all three at once, which is why a person can be drawn to it and frightened of it in the same hour.

tafsir_initial records a small point of language that a modern reader may find surprising. The Arabic word for thunder was ordinary vocabulary among the Arabs before Islam, and the Qur'an uses it in that ordinary sense in another sūrah: (13:13 — **“the thunder glorifies Him with praise, and so do the angels out of awe of Him”**). Some commentators transmitted a report traced to Ibn ʿAbbās describing thunder as the name of an angel placed over the clouds, and other scholars treated that report as an addition the text does not need, since the word was already known to every Arab ear. The lesson is worth keeping: a sound word does not need a strange story to give it weight.

**FINGERS IN THE EARS**

and with the terror the storm brings: ***“and lightning. They press their fingers into their ears at the sound of every thunder-clap”*** ***“for fear of death.”*** The scene is deliberately physical, and the commentators keep it that way. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the men as plugging their ears because of the crash of thunder, out of fear of death. Al-Jalālayn adds that they are cautious of dying. Nobody in that picture is being accused of stupidity about the weather. They are being shown as men who will do anything to avoid hearing the thing that carries a threat, even while they walk through the country the threat is about.

The commentators then map the gesture onto the behaviour of the hypocrites and the Jews of Madinah, who stopped their ears when the Qur'an was recited with its promises and its warnings. Tazkirul Qur'an draws out the reason in a way that speaks to any generation. Fear is another thing that keeps a person from acknowledging the truth. Rain is a blessing, but it arrives with thunder and lightning, and a faint-hearted man runs from the blessing because of the company it keeps. Accepting God's word does the same thing to a person's life. It puts his position at risk for a while. He may clash with the people around him and with the way everyone else is doing things. So he stops where he is, or he moves one hesitant step, and the caution does not save him from anything at all. Those who do not answer the call with an open heart lose the blessing they were afraid to reach for.

Kashānī describes the inner mechanics of the same gesture. The man keeps away from listening because the message would cut him off from the pleasures he is living for, and from where he stands, giving those up feels the same as dying. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps the picture in view for the reader: the covering of the ears answers to turning away from the truth and refusing to act on it.

Think of a town under a flood warning, with the siren going in the square and every door still shut. The people inside have not been misled about the water. They have simply decided that they would rather not hear about it.

**THE ONE WHO ENCOMPASSES THEM**

The verse then states the fact the men have forgotten: ***“And Allah encompasses the disbelievers ˹by His might˺”***. Al-Mukhtaṣar explains the closing phrase: God surrounds the disbelievers and has complete power over them, and there is no way for them to escape Him. The word for encompassing is the word for something being closed in on all sides. It is the answer to the ears and the fingers and the turning away. A man can refuse to hear a warning. He cannot walk out of the country the warning is about.

That closing line also holds the door open for the rest of the sūrah. The men in the storm are described with the word disbelievers in this verse, though the passage began with hypocrites, and the commentators are comfortable with the overlap. Hypocrisy of this kind is disbelief wearing a name. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the same phrase ends the verse that comes after this one, where God says He could have taken away their hearing and their sight, and the repetition ties the two verses together.

The verse also carries a small grammatical detail that al-Qurṭubī discusses at length, because it shows how the Qur'an handles repeated scenes. The little word for when that opens the description points to a future or repeated event, so the picture is not one storm on one afternoon. It is the standing condition of a certain kind of person, and the Book tells the reader what to expect when he meets him in any century.

---
## Verse 2:20

> It is as if the lightning were about to snatch away their sight. Whenever lightning strikes, they walk in its light, but when darkness covers them, they stand still. Had Allah willed, He could have taken away their hearing and sight. Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything

**THE LIGHT THAT ALMOST TOOK THEIR SIGHT**

The picture sharpens: ***“It is as if the lightning were about to snatch away their sight.”*** Al-Qurṭubī opens his discussion of this verse with the verb the translation renders as about to. In Arabic it means a thing is close to happening, so close that it has nearly begun, and the choice of word keeps the flash in the air. He notes the same construction in another sūrah: (24:43 — **“the flash of its lightning almost takes away the eyesight”**), where the same near-miss is described. The men are not being struck blind. They are being shown how close they are standing to a light they cannot handle.

Al-Jalālayn reads the phrase as the light taking their sight away swiftly, if it were given the chance. Al-Mukhtaṣar says the brightness almost blinds them. Ibn Kathīr places the whole scene inside the inner life: the sūrah is describing hypocrites who recognise the truth in some moments and doubt it in others, so that when the doubt and confusion come their hearts are like the rainstorm. The lightning is the moment of clarity, and the near-blindness is what clarity does to a man who has spent his life looking away.

**WALKING BY WHATEVER LIGHT ARRIVES**

The men take what the storm gives them: ***“Whenever lightning strikes, they walk in its light,”*** Al-Jalālayn gives the movement plainly: whenever the flash gives them light, they walk in it. Ibn ʿAbbās reads the same clause as the moment the hypocrites declared themselves believers and travelled along with the community for as long as the outward faith was accepted. That is the rhythm of the whole comparison. The light comes, the men move, and the moving is real while it lasts.

Al-Jalālayn then adds the observation that turns the weather into a portrait of the mind. The lightning stands for the arguments of the Qur'an as they strike a heart, and the men's response is mixed. They acknowledge the truths they happen to like and they pull back from the ones they dislike. That is a person choosing from a menu rather than submitting to a message. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps the movement on the ground: whenever the lightning flashes and lights things up, they go forward.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān describes the same people from the outside: they move a step or two when the flash comes, and when the sky goes dark again they stop where they are. A man who walks only in the light of someone else's flash is not on a road of his own choosing. He is being carried forward in short bursts by a thing he cannot control, and the verse leaves him exactly there.

**STANDING STILL WHEN THE LIGHT GOES**

and when it stops they stop: ***“but when darkness covers them, they stand still.”*** ***“Had Allah willed, He could have taken away their hearing”*** Al-Jalālayn reads the clause as men who stop and stand where they are. The Arabic describes a person rooted to the spot, and the sūrah uses the same verb elsewhere for the moment a thing simply comes to rest. Al-Mukhtaṣar says they remain in the darkness, unable to move.

The commentators apply this half to the moments after the flash passes: doubt returns, the community's arguments stop pressing on them, and the men do nothing at all. They do not go back, and they do not go on. Ibn Kathīr treats this hesitation as the defining mark of the second group among the hypocrites, the ones who know the truth and cannot bring themselves to live by it. Tazkirul Qur'an describes them in the same terms: fear of losing what they have, and fear of clashing with what everyone else does, keeps them from answering God's call with an open heart.

Notice how carefully the sūrah has drawn the difference between the two pictures it gives in these verses. The man in the fire parable loses the light altogether and stands in the dark. The travellers in the storm lose it and get it back, over and over, until the storm ends. One has lost the road. The other is being worn down by it.

**THE POWER THAT HOLDS THE WHOLE SCENE**

***“and sight.”*** The verse closes on the power behind the whole scene: ***“Surely Allah is Most Capable of everything”***. Al-Jalālayn reads the clause as a statement of what God could have done with the outer faculties in the same way He had already done with the inner ones. They hear the thunder and see the lightning with working organs, and the verse reminds them that the organs are on loan. Al-Mukhtaṣar adds the reason the phrase is there at all: God has power over all things, and if He took their hearing and sight away, He would not be handing them back, because they keep turning away from the truth.

Ibn ʿAbbās reads the words in the setting of the whole comparison. The thunder stands for the rebukes in the Qur'an and the lightning for its instructions and promises, and if God so willed, He could put an end to both, just as He could put an end to the hearing and the sight of the hypocrites and the Jews who kept their distance from the message. The verse takes the two faculties the men were using to keep the truth at arm's length — the ear they blocked and the eye they turned — and reminds them who made both.

There is comfort in the sentence as well, and the commentators do not miss it. The faculties are still in place. The thunder still comes, and the flashes still come, and the men in the storm are still being addressed. Kashānī describes the state as one of a man whose inner eye is clouded by sleep, so that the light of guidance reaches him and lifts him a little, and then the weight comes back. The verse says that the lifting and the weight are both in the hands of the One who could end the story whenever He chose.

Think of a driver on a road where the streetlights keep failing. When a lamp catches, he moves. When the road goes black, he stops and waits in the middle of the lane. He is still on the road, and he is still the reason he is not further along it.

**WHY THE PARABLE ENDS ON POWER**

Al-Mukhtaṣar reads the closing sentence as the ground of everything the passage has said: God has absolute power over all things. The men who cannot be moved by argument, the light that comes and goes, the hearing and the sight that could be withdrawn in a moment — all of it sits under one authority. The sūrah closes its account of the hypocrites with the widest possible statement about the One who is dealing with them.

The phrase also ends the introduction, and a reader can see the shape it leaves behind. Verses one to five presented the believers, and ended with the successful. Verses six and seven presented the open rejecters, and ended with a tremendous punishment. Verses eight to twenty presented the men with two faces, and end with power over everything. The next verse begins with a call addressed to all people, and the Book starts again from the beginning, speaking now to whoever is listening.

Ibn Kathīr notes that the two comparisons used in these last verses are the ones the sūrah will keep returning to as the chapter goes on. Hypocrisy of this kind does not end with one conversation in one city. It is a habit of mind that shows up whenever a person wants the benefits of faith without the surrender faith asks for. The sūrah names the condition on its opening page, and then spends its length teaching the reader what to do with it.

## Verse 2:187

> It has been made permissible for you to be intimate with your wives during the nights preceding the fast. Your spouses are a garment for you as you are for them. Allah knows that you were deceiving yourselves. So He has accepted your repentance and pardoned you. So now you may be intimate with them and seek what Allah has prescribed for you. ˹You may˺ eat and drink until you see the light of dawn breaking the darkness of night, then complete the fast until nightfall. Do not be intimate with your spouses while you are meditating in the mosques. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not exceed them. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to people, so they may become mindful ˹of Him˺

## Verse 2:196

> Complete the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage for Allah. But if prevented ˹from proceeding˺, then ˹offer˺ whatever sacrificial animals you can afford. And do not shave your heads until the sacrificial animal reaches its destination. But if any of you is ill or has a scalp ailment ˹requiring shaving˺, then compensate either by fasting, charity, or a sacrificial offering. In times of peace, you may combine the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage then make the sacrificial offering you can afford. Whoever cannot afford that ˹offering˺, let them fast three days during pilgrimage and seven after returning ˹home˺—completing ten. These offerings are for those who do not live near the Sacred House. And be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is severe in punishment

## Verse 2:217

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about fighting in the sacred months. Say, “Fighting during these months is a great sin. But hindering ˹others˺ from the Path of Allah, rejecting Him, and expelling the worshippers from the Sacred Mosque is ˹a˺ greater ˹sin˺ in the sight of Allah. For persecution is far worse than killing. And they will not stop fighting you until they turn you away from your faith—if they can. And whoever among you renounces their own faith and dies a disbeliever, their deeds will become void in this life and in the Hereafter. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about menstruation. Say, “Beware of its harm! So keep away, and do not have intercourse with your wives during their monthly cycles until they are purified. When they purify themselves, then you may approach them in the manner specified by Allah. Surely Allah loves those who always turn to Him in repentance and those who purify themselves.”

## Verse 2:228

> Divorced women must wait three monthly cycles ˹before they can re-marry˺. It is not lawful for them to conceal what Allah has created in their wombs, if they ˹truly˺ believe in Allah and the Last Day. And their husbands reserve the right to take them back within that period if they desire reconciliation. Women have rights similar to those of men equitably, although men have a degree ˹of responsibility˺ above them. And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise

## Verse 2:229

> Divorce may be retracted twice, then the husband must retain ˹his wife˺ with honour or separate ˹from her˺ with grace. It is not lawful for husbands to take back anything of the dowry given to their wives, unless the couple fears not being able to keep within the limits of Allah. So if you fear they will not be able to keep within the limits of Allah, there is no blame if the wife compensates the husband to obtain divorce. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not transgress them. And whoever transgresses the limits of Allah, they are the ˹true˺ wrongdoers

## Verse 2:233

> ˹Divorced˺ mothers will breastfeed their offspring for two whole years, for those who wish to complete the nursing ˹of their child˺. The child’s father will provide reasonable maintenance and clothing for the mother ˹during that period˺. No one will be charged with more than they can bear. No mother or father should be made to suffer for their child. The ˹father’s˺ heirs are under the same obligation. But if both sides decide—after mutual consultation and consent—to wean a child, then there is no blame on them. If you decide to have your children nursed by a wet-nurse, it is permissible as long as you pay fairly. Be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

## Verse 2:259

> Or ˹are you not aware of˺ the one who passed by a city which was in ruins. He wondered, “How could Allah bring this back to life after its destruction?” So Allah caused him to die for a hundred years then brought him back to life. Allah asked, “How long have you remained ˹in this state˺?” He replied, “Perhaps a day or part of a day.” Allah said, “No! You have remained here for a hundred years! Just look at your food and drink—they have not spoiled. ˹But now˺ look at ˹the remains of˺ your donkey! And ˹so˺ We have made you into a sign for humanity. And look at the bones ˹of the donkey˺, how We bring them together then clothe them with flesh!” When this was made clear to him, he declared, “˹Now˺ I know that Allah is Most Capable of everything.”

## Verse 2:275

> Those who consume interest will stand ˹on Judgment Day˺ like those driven to madness by Satan’s touch. That is because they say, “Trade is no different than interest.” But Allah has permitted trading and forbidden interest. Whoever refrains—after having received warning from their Lord—may keep their previous gains, and their case is left to Allah. As for those who persist, it is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever

## Verse 2:282

> O believers! When you contract a loan for a fixed period of time, commit it to writing. Let the scribe maintain justice between the parties. The scribe should not refuse to write as Allah has taught them to write. They will write what the debtor dictates, bearing Allah in mind and not defrauding the debt. If the debtor is incompetent, weak, or unable to dictate, let their guardian dictate for them with justice. Call upon two of your men to witness. If two men cannot be found, then one man and two women of your choice will witness—so if one of the women forgets the other may remind her. The witnesses must not refuse when they are summoned. You must not be against writing ˹contracts˺ for a fixed period—whether the sum is small or great. This is more just ˹for you˺ in the sight of Allah, and more convenient to establish evidence and remove doubts. However, if you conduct an immediate transaction among yourselves, then there is no need for you to record it, but call upon witnesses when a deal is finalized. Let no harm come to the scribe or witnesses. If you do, then you have gravely exceeded ˹your limits˺. Be mindful of Allah, for Allah ˹is the One Who˺ teaches you. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

## Verse 2:286

> Allah does not require of any soul more than what it can afford. All good will be for its own benefit, and all evil will be to its own loss. ˹The believers pray,˺ “Our Lord! Do not punish us if we forget or make a mistake. Our Lord! Do not place a burden on us like the one you placed on those before us. Our Lord! Do not burden us with what we cannot bear. Pardon us, forgive us, and have mercy on us. You are our ˹only˺ Guardian. So grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”
